ParlaMint-SI_2017-01-26-SDZ7-Redna-26.ana.u216	The Minister of the Interior has the floor, Mag. Vesna Györkös Žnidar. Here you go.
ParlaMint-SI_2017-01-26-SDZ7-Redna-26.ana.u217	Thank you for your word, Vice President. I will begin where we started today's debate. The first observation expected, of course, was that this law was to be unconstitutional. Some of the more precise explanations, in which or why this law should be unconstitutional, have not been heard today. We have heard the main moral criticism, but I believe that the very explanation of the law is so good, also supported by the implementation of the Government's Law Office's representative on the recent committee. At this point, I think it is enough to say that the position of the Government is that we are subject to restrictions within the limits of the Constitution, that is, those restrictions which the Constitution allows, in the fact that the Constitution expressly stipulates that the right to asylum is guaranteed within the limits of the law, on the fact that also on the basis of the amendment of the Aliens Act, certain vulnerable groups of foreigners will still be adopted. However, most importantly, when the measure comes into force, in accordance with the amendment of the Foreign Law, it will not affect all those who will seek international protection against the application of this measure; nor will it affect those who will have already recognised the status of international protection in the Republic of Slovenia at that time. And those areas where this measure does not apply will be exempted. It is also important that the law is directed to prevent an emergency or to prevent threats to public order and internal security. And as has been pointed out several times in the past days, we are of the view that waiting for a state of emergency or a threat to internal security is unconstitutional. In a state of emergency, which is also a very difficult category from a constitutional point of view, with all the consequences that such a situation entails when the existence of a State which is a presumptive state of emergency is threatened, it is indeed difficult, if not impossible, to guarantee other human rights, including the right to asylum. I do not think that this interpretation is based on any convention, nor can any organisation in Slovenia suggest that it must interpret any legal basis in this way. Amendments to the Aliens Act are also the legal basis for action, that Slovenia does not collapse and that it prevents the deterioration of internal security and state of emergency. As regards the final assessment of constitutionality, whether the law is constitutional or not, we have already said this several times, only the Constitutional Court can say that. If this law is found on the desks of the judges of the Constitutional Court, this will also give direction to Slovenia's proceedings in case of overload. If there is a breaking point, which can then pose a threat to public order and internal security, although I believe that it is not ideologically acceptable for certain individuals to do so, but on such difficult issues as the face of migration pressure, it must be based primarily on the rule of law. Perhaps a few further clarifications were made about the procession that led to this law’s arrival in the National Assembly. It is quite clear that I am acting today as a representative of the Government and that the Government has proposed legislation or that it proposes any measures. The Office of the Government of Law has also examined this law before the adoption of the law by the Government from the point of view of the article of the Constitution, which was mentioned in the introduction today, i.e. Article 153 of the Constitution. It is quite clear that laws must comply with the Constitution, this is an essential legal matter. The Office of the Government of Law did not find any confusion, unconstitutionality and irregularities, and the Government has therefore also confirmed this law in all the arguments that I have also submitted. On the Committee, a representative of the Government's Law Service, under the authority of the Secretary-General of the Government, provided additional explanations or presented the content of the Government's position in legal terms. Today, however, we are in the legislative process where this very valuable weighting is carried out. The views of the Government are clear, but it is also clear that only the Constitutional Court can give a definitive assessment of whether the law is in accordance with the Constitution or not. If that's not true, then we don't need a Constitutional Court. Nor is it true that what has been said today is based on some assumption that refugees pose a threat, that is not true. The fact is that any link between migration flows and migrants to a state of emergency, which was an alternative to the formation of this law, which we did not agree on, and these attempts to link the emergency to migration are the securitisation of a couple of excellence. And we did not agree with this because, in fact, this is how migrants are then defined as an existential threat to a country that would require some extraordinary measures. This is another step beyond this framework on which the Aliens Act is based, when migration is still referred to as a classic, as a standard political issue, which, of course, also contains security elements and also requires an appropriate and timely response from the country. Migration is also a security issue, not just a humanitarian issue. I think that we have demonstrated in the past that we are aware of all aspects and that we have taken a comprehensive approach to the migration crisis. As far as the chronology of this law is concerned, there is also much talk about it. Officially, this law came into government procedure last fall. But here I have to add another essential circumstance, we haven't talked much about it, but we've already talked about it in public. I can tell you that this law at the Ministry of the Interior has been on the table since autumn 2015, and I can say that as soon as the next day after the European Commission's visit, when we were directly offered to make Slovenia a hot spot, that is, a hot spot. Since then, all the red lights have been on at the Ministry, all those that have not yet been lit at the moment the migration flow has been diverted to Slovenia. After some unsuccessful attempts to change the normative basis – because the normative basis is also one way to counter or control migration flows, not only measures on the ground, but also legislative bases are essential because Slovenia is a legal state – today this law is before you for one simple reason because we are responsible for security. When Slovenia becomes a pocket, it's too late. The primary problem in this whole complex situation is not the closure of the border by Austria, which is in fact just one of the manifestations of national measures, because there is still no European solution. We know she's not here yet. It is also a problem for Slovenia because it affects Slovenia, but the key problem is that the external borders of the European Union are porous. Of course, we also did not forget the double policies that took place during the migration wave, i.e. the declaratory open borders, and in practice the closure of borders, which also caused enormous problems for our security authorities, who faced extremely difficult situations daily and also made great efforts to control the situation at the end of the day. I do not agree with the conclusion that the Aliens Act would endanger internal security because this law carries an explicit preventive component. If we read carefully, Article 10a states: “If the Ministry of the Interior, on the basis of the information provided by the authorities and institutions referred to in the preceding paragraph, considers that conditions could or have already arisen in the Republic of Slovenia”, it could therefore be that “the changing situation in the field of migration would endanger public order and internal security...” it is clear that this law is aimed at not reaching the pocket. It's not like we're making a pocket with this law. Even before the government's hearing, SNAV is the one who gives the opinion. If we narrow this situation down to a very short summary, today we are deciding how to deal with the situation when we are no longer able to implement the International Protection Act, when in Slovenia, i.e. in the territory of the Republic of Slovenia, a large number of foreigners coming across the five safe countries coming across the two external borders of the European Union are unwilling to ask for international protection, and they will not be able to continue because the countries in the north are closing their borders. Our proposal, as we say, stems from all those restrictions contained in the Constitution. We propose amendments to prevent an emergency, because this is our constitutional duty. An emergency cannot be part of a single government's solution, because that would be extremely irresponsible. It is also my firm position that no one can command the Republic of Slovenia that the problem of the European Union concerning migration should be resolved in Slovenia, because Slovenia is not or must not be victims of the fact that the European Union has fallen asleep in the resolution of the migration crisis. This crisis has only escalated in 2015 and has been hot for quite some time. The porous external borders of the European Union are not the fault of the Republic of Slovenia. Today we have also talked a lot about all these letters and visits by these all-powerful institutions at all different levels, European and international, but I think that the primary focus of all these institutions should be to get to the common asylum policy as soon as possible, not to act as a judge or to deal with those national solutions to which countries are actually forced because there is no common solution. This is certainly not the right way; it signals that all these organizations have not done their job, because if they did, they would certainly not be discussing these problems today. Today’s session also confirms in some ways that this problem has been pushed under the rug for too long, but we know how it will eventually come to the surface. The talk was also that we did not explain what the burden is, what objective restrictions are. I don't agree with that. We've talked about this a lot. The criteria are clear in marriage. An assessment based on cumulative orientation or focus, in accordance with what is stated in the law, the Government then proposes to the National Assembly by means of SNAV. Of course, it also depends on the situation, because this situation still has many unpredictable elements. The law is written for emergencies. However, it is important that Slovenia implements the Law on International Protection by the time we assess that we are capable. It is also important that countries, with regard to their potential for international protection, which is also extremely important and is one of the fundamental achievements of civilisation, cannot be given to a common denominator on this potential. The Geneva Convention, if you read carefully, also speaks of the first safe country. There is something wrong with this existing system if there is still a migration wave across so many safe countries and across two external borders of the European Union that is mixed, in which not only those people who are fleeing persecution within the meaning of the Geneva Convention are travelling. It is also important that what the state can do depends on the ability of the domestic political environment. And it is essential that we also begin to talk about where the limit is, what is still what Slovenia can do, and what is the legal basis that is necessary in the event of a situation where things can become uncontrollable. I do not think it is constructive just to discuss in the direction of some absolute concepts, but Slovenia must accept the requests of all foreigners who come to the territory of the Republic of Slovenia at any time. The crisis in 2015 revealed one very obvious dimension, since never before, as far as the European Union is concerned, migration was not so close, so closely linked to the concept of international protection, even though these two concepts were separate. And this is one of the fundamental anomalies of the Geneva Convention in such a complex situation, because it causes less aid or less international protection at the end of the day for those who obviously need it and who need it immediately. The European Commission has also made clear its views. This is also the case with our law that there must be no right to choose the country in which international protection is requested, because this is also a factor in making it difficult for countries with limited capacity to confront. The objective fact is that it is mandatory to register that it is mandatory to take fingerprints and that migration flows management must take place at the external borders of the European Union in accordance with European Union legislation as well as European Union standards, as confirmed by this migration crisis and also by terrorist threats which are not to be ignored. Of course, internal security is inseparably linked to today's debate. Internal security has been a fundamental driving force for re-temporary control, which has been re-entered at the Slovenia-Austria border. The police are guarding the Schengen border. In fact, in this situation, as we are, the Schengen de facto fell apart. It is precisely internal security, which means that Slovenia must do everything or take all measures to ensure its internal security, because we know what it means for Slovenia to leave the Schengen area. Schengen is our national interest as small countries. And it is precisely for the sake of ensuring internal security that we are obliged to weigh between humanitarianism on the one hand and the objective capabilities of the country on the other. Therefore, it is also a key issue, which we are also discussing today and which is actually addressed by this law; that is, when and how it cuts into the right of asylum when the state approaches such a situation, when public order and internal security can be seriously threatened, which can therefore affect the difficult exercise of the vital functions of the State or central institutions. This question is not only placed in our national context. However, we are a member of one international environment, but it is necessary to add here that international legal order must also adapt to the modern situation, because today's meeting, as has already been said, confirms that it is not an adequate situation with mass migration that is not identical to the concept of international protection. Here we must be aware that there are two separate concepts. When the Geneva Convention was adopted, those who monitored these materials a little more closely, even then, there was a talk about how, in the context of humanitarianism, which was in place at that time and is absolutely still today, they also faced threats to internal security. If you read the Geneva Convention carefully, it also contains these elements. And in modern times, i.e. at a time of globalisation and express mobility, which is a constant of today's time, but which was not at the time of adoption of the Geneva Convention, today the relationship to the security dimension is even more important, but not less important. As the security environment is changing, countries must have sovereign control over illegal migration, and of course it is also in the interest of potential refugees that international protection is properly regulated. However, the prerequisite for this is that we begin, in view of all these elements that I have listed, by changing the context, given the situation in the world when the Geneva Convention accepted that we are also dealing with country constraints and also with the question of practical feasibility. These issues are also addressed by Mr James C. Hathaway, whom you all the time refer to as some judge who has judged that what the government is proposing is an abominable move. But I must tell you that he is also talking about the need to harmonise moral criticism with practical constraints and that international law between countries is a consensually agreed legal order. If the legitimate limits of countries are not respected, it is not expected that international law will perform that function, which is of a humanitarian nature, because the declaration of formal standards is not sufficient. This is also Mr Hathaway's position. Whatever he has been informed that we are doing, I think he has very partial information, because if it were fully explained to him, he would certainly recall these positions, which are very much in place, because he calls for an international legal order that governs refugee law to be reviewed precisely to achieve optimal solutions that will be able to respond even more effectively to the challenges of modern times. Mass migration is one of the components that is important today; the asylum system is not something that could be likened to mass migration movements of people, but serves a completely different point. If the only correct interpretation was that Slovenia was the one where the European problem would be solved, then I am sure that Slovenia would not also sign the Geneva Convention. We talked a lot about the law. Perhaps just a little bit deeper to refresh our memory that it will be perfectly clear that this law is intended to manage an exceptional situation when we are no longer able to implement the Law on International Protection – not without not wanting to, but every country has its limitations, including Slovenia as the smallest country on the Western Balkan route – in a situation where public order and internal security as well as the functioning of the State and its subsystems may also be at risk. The law now clearly states that these are individualised registration procedures, which are also important for our safety. We need to know who is entering our country, of course, when checking all those circumstances that are essential to determine the potential vulnerability of persons; and when we are surrounded by the very safe Member States of the European Union, where there are no systemic shortcomings of asylum systems, because we are part of the common European asylum area and the rules are perfectly clear here. The law refers to a situation which Slovenia will have to adapt to in some bad, black scenarios; an exceptional situation when it is no longer possible to implement the law. It is possible to introduce exceptional measures under precisely defined conditions, criteria, assessments; on an important assumption that we are surrounded by the safe Member States themselves of the European Union, which means that there is no breach of the principle of non-refoulement, so no one would return to the country, potentially persecuted persons would not be returned to the country where their security or freedom would be compromised. Therefore, it is without prejudice to non-refoulement, without prejudice to the prohibition of torture, without prejudice to the duty to protect human life, dignity and all those rights which cannot be excluded under any circumstances. Maybe I'll go back to the Geneva Convention for the rest of my life. The fact is, there is absolutely no secret that countries have been having difficulties implementing this Convention for quite some time. This may be new for Slovenia because it is the first time it faces mass migration, but countries have been working for quite some time on the idea that international legal order needs to be revised. At the time the Convention was adopted, there was no problem with that classical treatment, individual treatment. But since there are very large numbers to change the nature and structure of migration flows, there are problems in practice. The basic concept of the Geneva Convention, which is based on the abandonment of countries as a solution to the refugee problem, is also a problem and this is also seen as an inappropriate solution in the international legal community. It is necessary to seek solutions based on the individual’s right to remain in his country or to return home or to enjoy all the fundamental human rights that all people should have. The Geneva Convention, as such, is under migratory pressure. Since this does not fully correspond to the current situation, this also causes applicants to be considered in the context of migration control, because this is something which is certainly beyond the requirement of non-refoulement. This is one problem that will need to be analysed from other perspectives. In particular, it should also be stressed that the Convention does not take into account the potential impact on host countries, either in financial, social or political terms. This is certainly one of the tasks with which not only Slovenia, but also the international community, must work intensively in the coming times to reach a system that is optimal for the protection of such important rights as the right to asylum.

We continue with the adjourned 5th item of the agenda, which is the third reading of the Proposal for a Law on Aid for the Rescue and Restructuring of Economic Companies and Cooperatives in difficulty under the ordinary procedure. We are moving on to a decision on the bill. I inform you that the Legislative and Legal Service or the Government has not warned me that, due to amendments adopted in the second reading at the home work body and at the meeting of the National Assembly, the proposal for a law would be inconsistent. We're voting. There are 64 Members and Members present, with 49 votes against 1. [[The vote was 49.]] [[Against one.]] I take it the law has been passed. I hereby conclude this agenda item. We continue with the 9th item of the agenda, which is the second reading of the draft law on pricing of petroleum derivatives under the regular procedure. We are moving on to a decision on the following proposal for a decision: The proposal for a law on pricing of petroleum derivatives is not suitable for further consideration. We're voting. There are 61 Members and Members present, with 57 votes against 2. [[The vote was 57.]] [[Against two.]] I note that the assembly has adopted a decision, and therefore the legislative procedure for the bill has been completed. I hereby conclude this agenda item and interrupt the 26th meeting of the National Assembly, which will be resumed tomorrow at 10 a.m.
